It just keeps me from getting slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I do. I mean, my husband and the apprentices at the shop keep saying I look too thin, but I just tell them I'm fine. Honestly, I think people are just confusing discipline with sickness.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. If I eat anything heavy, or... let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying right away. I just worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, never. I really don't eat much at one time at all. Honestly, it's the exact opposite. I keep my portions very small so I don't feel weighed down while I'm working. The thought of eating a large amount of food like that just... no, I don't do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Since I eat so little to begin with, there really isn't any reason to do something like that. So, no, I've never made myself throw up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor is the one who sent me here. Um, my weight has dropped pretty low recently, and everyone is just making a big fuss over it. But honestly, I don't really see the issue. I feel like being this light helps me keep up with the work in my shop. It just keeps me from getting slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I do. I mean, my husband and the apprentices at the shop keep saying I look too thin, but I just tell them I'm fine. Honestly, I think people are just confusing discipline with sickness.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. If I eat anything heavy, or... let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying right away. I just worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, never. I really don't eat much at one time at all. Honestly, it's the exact opposite. I keep my portions very small so I don't feel weighed down while I'm working. The thought of eating a large amount of food like that just... no, I don't do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Since I eat so little to begin with, there really isn't any reason to do something like that. So, no, I've never made myself throw up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape has anything to do with your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean... I've never really looked at it that way. I've been a carpenter for a long time, and I'd say my worth comes from the hard work I do and being good at my trade. So, no, I haven't noticed my weight or my shape having anything to do with my actual self-worth as a person. It's really just a strict standard I keep for myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be able to stop dieting or restricting your food intake if you reached a weight that your doctor or family considered healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't think I would. It's not really about what someone else considers healthy, whether it's a doctor or my family. This is just the routine I need to maintain for myself. I don't see myself just stopping or changing how I eat because someone else picked a weight for me. Like I said, it's my own standard, so I don't think I'd just drop it. It's just how I operate now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you eat and when you eat it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say I have total control. Um... I decide exactly what I eat and when I eat it. Like I mentioned before, it's a strict routine I keep, so... I don't ever really feel out of control with food, if that's what you're asking. I just stick to my standard and that's it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to eat more if your job as a carpenter didn't require you to be so active and mobile?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, my job keeps me moving, sure, but... the way I eat isn't really tied to how many hours I spend on a ladder or hauling wood. Even if I were sitting at a desk all day, I'd still stick to the same routine. It's just the standard I've set for myself, so being less active wouldn't make me want to eat more. It really wouldn't change anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to lose weight even if you were at a weight that most people would consider healthy\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. It's... well, it's just that I can't really let go of it. If I eat anything heavy, or even let myself have the foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away. I just immediately worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep. So, no matter what weight I'm at or what other people think is healthy, that fear of gaining is always there. I'd still want to keep the weight off just to keep that control.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I'm sure I still would. Like I said before, my job doesn't really dictate how I eat. It's more about the routine I've set for myself. So, even if I weren't doing carpentry, I'd still be doing the exact same thing with my food.

It really wouldn't change anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to lose weight even if you were at a weight that most people would consider healthy\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. It's... well, it's just that I can't really let go of it. If I eat anything heavy, or even let myself have the foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor is the one who sent me here. Um, my weight has dropped pretty low recently, and everyone is just making a big fuss over it. But honestly, I don't really see the issue. I feel like being this light helps me keep up with the work in my shop. It just keeps me from getting slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I do. I mean, my husband and the apprentices at the shop keep saying I look too thin, but I just tell them I'm fine. Honestly, I think people are just confusing discipline with sickness.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. If I eat anything heavy, or... let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying right away. I just worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, never. I really don't eat much at one time at all. Honestly, it's the exact opposite. I keep my portions very small so I don't feel weighed down while I'm working. The thought of eating a large amount of food like that just... no, I don't do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Since I eat so little to begin with, there really isn't any reason to do something like that. I've never really looked at it that way. I've been a carpenter for a long time, and I'd say my worth comes from the hard work I do and being good at my trade. So, no, I haven't noticed my weight or my shape having anything to do with my actual self-worth as a person. It's really just a strict standard I keep for myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be able to stop dieting or restricting your food intake if you reached a weight that your doctor or family considered healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't think I would. It's not really about what someone else considers healthy, whether it's a doctor or my family. This is just the routine I need to maintain for myself. I don't see myself just stopping or changing how I eat because someone else picked a weight for me. Like I said, it's my own standard, so I don't think I'd just drop it. It's just how I operate now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you eat and when you eat it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say I have total control. Um... I just stick to my standard and that's it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to eat more if your job as a carpenter didn't require you to be so active and mobile?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor is the one who sent me here. Um, my weight has dropped pretty low recently, and everyone is just making a big fuss over it. But honestly, I don't really see the issue. I feel like being this light helps me keep up with the work in my shop. It just keeps me from getting slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I do. I mean, my husband and the apprentices at the shop keep saying I look too thin, but I just tell them I'm fine. Honestly, I think people are just confusing discipline with sickness.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. If I eat anything heavy, or... let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying right away. I just worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, never. I really don't eat much at one time at all. Honestly, it's the exact opposite. I keep my portions very small so I don't feel weighed down while I'm working. The thought of eating a large amount of food like that just... no, I don't do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Since I eat so little to begin with, there really isn't any reason to do something like that. So, no, I've never made myself throw up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape has anything to do with your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean... I've never really looked at it that way. I've been a carpenter for a long time, and I'd say my worth comes from the hard work I do and being good at my trade. So, no, I haven't noticed my weight or my shape having anything to do with my actual self-worth as a person. It's really just a strict standard I keep for myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be able to stop dieting or restricting your food intake if you reached a weight that your doctor or family considered healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't think I would. It's not really about what someone else considers healthy, whether it's a doctor or my family. This is just the routine I need to maintain for myself. I don't see myself just stopping or changing how I eat because someone else picked a weight for me. Like I said, it's my own standard, so I don't think I'd just drop it. It's just how I operate now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you eat and when you eat it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say I have total control. Um... I decide exactly what I eat and when I eat it. Like I mentioned before, it's a strict routine I keep, so... I don't ever really feel out of control with food, if that's what you're asking. I just stick to my standard and that's it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to eat more if your job as a carpenter didn't require you to be so active and mobile?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, my job keeps me moving, sure, but... the way I eat isn't really tied to how many hours I spend on a ladder or hauling wood. Even if I were sitting at a desk all day, I'd still stick to the same routine. It's just the standard I've set for myself, so being less active wouldn't make me want to eat more. It really wouldn't change anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to lose weight even if you were at a weight that most people would consider healthy\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. It's... well, it's just that I can't really let go of it. If I eat anything heavy, or even let myself have the foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away. I just immediately worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep. So, no matter what weight I'm at or what other people think is healthy, that fear of gaining is always there. I'd still want to keep the weight off just to keep that control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to restrict your food intake if you weren't a carpenter and didn't have the physical demands of that job?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm sure I still would. Like I said before, my job doesn't really dictate how I eat. It's more about the routine I've set for myself. On workshop days, I usually just have tea and maybe a few careful bites of something. Stopping for a real meal just feels unnecessary, and honestly, it throws off the control I've got over the day. So, even if I weren't doing carpentry, I'd still be doing the exact same thing with my food.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I think they're really the same thing for me. Like I mentioned a few minutes ago, if I eat something heavy, or even think about having those rich foods we used to make for the holidays, I just... I start worrying right away that I'll gain weight. And if I gain weight, I feel like I'll lose that grip I've worked so hard to keep. So avoiding the weight gain is basically how I hold onto that control.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about not wanting to feel fat or more about maintaining a sense of control over your daily life?\n\nPatient: It's... well, it's really both, but they tie together. Like I said before, if I eat anything heavy, or if I even think about having those rich holiday foods we used to make, I just start worrying right away that I'm going to gain weight. And if I gain weight, it feels like I'm losing that grip I've worked so hard to keep. So the fear of gaining weight and the need for control are basically the same thing to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, my job keeps me moving, sure, but... the way I eat isn't really tied to how many hours I spend on a ladder or hauling wood. Even if I were sitting at a desk all day, I'd still stick to the same routine. It's just the standard I've set for myself, so being less active wouldn't make me want to eat more. It really wouldn't change anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to lose weight even if you were at a weight that most people would consider healthy\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. It's... well, it's just that I can't really let go of it. If I eat anything heavy, or even let myself have the foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away. I just immediately worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep. So, no matter what weight I'm at or what other people think is healthy, that fear of gaining is always there. I'd still want to keep the weight off just to keep that control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to restrict your food intake if you weren't a carpenter and didn't have the physical demands of that job?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm sure I still would. Like I said before, my job doesn't really dictate how I eat. It's more about the routine I've set for myself. On workshop days, I usually just have tea and maybe a few careful bites of something. Stopping for a real meal just feels unnecessary, and honestly, it throws off the control I've got over the day. So, even if I weren't doing carpentry, I'd still be doing the exact same thing with my food.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I think they're really the same thing for me. Like I mentioned a few minutes ago, if I eat something heavy, or even think about having those rich foods we used to make for the holidays, I just... I start worrying right away that I'll gain weight. And if I gain weight, I feel like I'll lose that grip I've worked so hard to keep. So avoiding the weight gain is basically how I hold onto that control.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about not wanting to feel fat or more about maintaining a sense of control over your daily life?\n\nPatient: It's... well, it's really both, but they tie together. Like I said before, if I eat anything heavy, or if I even think about having those rich holiday foods we used to make, I just start worrying right away that I'm going to gain weight. And if I gain weight, it feels like I'm losing that grip I've worked so hard to keep. So the fear of gaining weight and the need for control are basically the same thing to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in your life, besides your weight, that you feel you need to have strict control over?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't think so. I mean, my work has to be precise, obviously, since I'm a carpenter. You have to measure things right and be careful. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor is the one who sent me here. Um, my weight has dropped pretty low recently, and everyone is just making a big fuss over it. But honestly, I don't really see the issue. I feel like being this light helps me keep up with the work in my shop. It just keeps me from getting slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I do. I mean, my husband and the apprentices at the shop keep saying I look too thin, but I just tell them I'm fine. Honestly, I think people are just confusing discipline with sickness.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. If I eat anything heavy, or... let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying right away. I just worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, never. I really don't eat much at one time at all. Honestly, it's the exact opposite. I keep my portions very small so I don't feel weighed down while I'm working. The thought of eating a large amount of food like that just... no, I don't do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Since I eat so little to begin with, there really isn't any reason to do something like that. So, no, I've never made myself throw up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape has anything to do with your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean... I've never really looked at it that way. I've been a carpenter for a long time, and I'd say my worth comes from the hard work I do and being good at my trade. So, no, I haven't noticed my weight or my shape having anything to do with my actual self-worth as a person. It's really just a strict standard I keep for myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be able to stop dieting or restricting your food intake if you reached a weight that your doctor or family considered healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't think I would. It's not really about what someone else considers healthy, whether it's a doctor or my family. This is just the routine I need to maintain for myself. I don't see myself just stopping or changing how I eat because someone else picked a weight for me. Like I said, it's my own standard, so I don't think I'd just drop it. It's just how I operate now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you eat and when you eat it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say I have total control. Um... I decide exactly what I eat and when I eat it. Like I mentioned before, it's a strict routine I keep, so... I don't ever really feel out of control with food, if that's what you're asking. I just stick to my standard and that's it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to eat more if your job as a carpenter didn't require you to be so active and mobile?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, my job keeps me moving, sure, but... the way I eat isn't really tied to how many hours I spend on a ladder or hauling wood. Even if I were sitting at a desk all day, I'd still stick to the same routine. It's just the standard I've set for myself, so being less active wouldn't make me want to eat more. It really wouldn't change anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to lose weight even if you were at a weight that most people would consider healthy\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. It's... well, it's just that I can't really let go of it. If I eat anything heavy, or even let myself have the foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away. I just immediately worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep. So, no matter what weight I'm at or what other people think is healthy, that fear of gaining is always there. I'd still want to keep the weight off just to keep that control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to restrict your food intake if you weren't a carpenter and didn't have the physical demands of that job?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm sure I still would. Like I said before, my job doesn't really dictate how I eat. It's more about the routine I've set for myself. On workshop days, I usually just have tea and maybe a few careful bites of something. Stopping for a real meal just feels unnecessary, and honestly, it throws off the control I've got over the day. So, even if I weren't doing carpentry, I'd still be doing the exact same thing with my food.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I think they're really the same thing for me. Like I mentioned a few minutes ago, if I eat something heavy, or even think about having those rich foods we used to make for the holidays, I just... I start worrying right away that I'll gain weight. And if I gain weight, I feel like I'll lose that grip I've worked so hard to keep. So avoiding the weight gain is basically how I hold onto that control.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about not wanting to feel fat or more about maintaining a sense of control over your daily life?\n\nPatient: It's... well, it's really both, but they tie together. Like I said before, if I eat anything heavy, or if I even think about having those rich holiday foods we used to make, I just start worrying right away that I'm going to gain weight. And if I gain weight, it feels like I'm losing that grip I've worked so hard to keep. So the fear of gaining weight and the need for control are basically the same thing to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in your life, besides your weight, that you feel you need to have strict control over?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't think so. I mean, my work has to be precise, obviously, since I'm a carpenter. You have to measure things right and be careful. But that's just doing the job properly. \n\nOutside of that... no, I haven't really noticed anything else. It's really just the food and my weight that I feel like I have to keep that tight grip on. Everything else in my life is just, you know, normal. I don't feel that same need to control it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see yourself as overweight even if other people told you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, people don't really tell me I'm underweight, so I haven't really thought about it like that. I just look in the mirror and see what I see. I guess... I'm not sure if what other people say would change how I see myself. I just know I still feel like I have weight to lose.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's... well, it's just that I can't really let go of it. If I eat anything heavy, or even let myself have the foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away. I just immediately worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep. So, no matter what weight I'm at or what other people think is healthy, that fear of gaining is always there. I'd still want to keep the weight off just to keep that control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to restrict your food intake if you weren't a carpenter and didn't have the physical demands of that job?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm sure I still would. Like I said before, my job doesn't really dictate how I eat. It's more about the routine I've set for myself. On workshop days, I usually just have tea and maybe a few careful bites of something. Stopping for a real meal just feels unnecessary, and honestly, it throws off the control I've got over the day. So, even if I weren't doing carpentry, I'd still be doing the exact same thing with my food.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I think they're really the same thing for me. Like I mentioned a few minutes ago, if I eat something heavy, or even think about having those rich foods we used to make for the holidays, I just... I start worrying right away that I'll gain weight. And if I gain weight, I feel like I'll lose that grip I've worked so hard to keep. So avoiding the weight gain is basically how I hold onto that control.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about not wanting to feel fat or more about maintaining a sense of control over your daily life?\n\nPatient: It's... well, it's really both, but they tie together. Like I said before, if I eat anything heavy, or if I even think about having those rich holiday foods we used to make, I just start worrying right away that I'm going to gain weight. And if I gain weight, it feels like I'm losing that grip I've worked so hard to keep. So the fear of gaining weight and the need for control are basically the same thing to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in your life, besides your weight, that you feel you need to have strict control over?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't think so. I mean, my work has to be precise, obviously, since I'm a carpenter. You have to measure things right and be careful. But that's just doing the job properly. \n\nOutside of that... no, I haven't really noticed anything else. It's really just the food and my weight that I feel like I have to keep that tight grip on. Everything else in my life is just, you know, normal. I don't feel that same need to control it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see yourself as overweight even if other people told you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, people don't really tell me I'm underweight, so I haven't really thought about it like that. I just look in the mirror and see what I see. I guess... I'm not sure if what other people say would change how I see myself. I just know I still feel like I have weight to lose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than a healthy weight for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't think so. I mean... my husband and the apprentices at work keep saying I look too thin lately. But I just tell them I'm fine. I honestly think people are just confusing discipline with sickness. I work hard to keep myself in check, so no, I don't see it as being lower than a healthy weight at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It just keeps me from getting slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I do. I mean, my husband and the apprentices at the shop keep saying I look too thin, but I just tell them I'm fine. If I eat anything heavy, or... let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying right away. I just worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, never. I really don't eat much at one time at all. Honestly, it's the exact opposite. I keep my portions very small so I don't feel weighed down while I'm working. Since I eat so little to begin with, there really isn't any reason to do something like that. I've never really looked at it that way. I've been a carpenter for a long time, and I'd say my worth comes from the hard work I do and being good at my trade. So, no, I haven't noticed my weight or my shape having anything to do with my actual self-worth as a person. It's really just a strict standard I keep for myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be able to stop dieting or restricting your food intake if you reached a weight that your doctor or family considered healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't think I would. It's not really about what someone else considers healthy, whether it's a doctor or my family. This is just the routine I need to maintain for myself. I don't see myself just stopping or changing how I eat because someone else picked a weight for me. Like I said, it's my own standard, so I don't think I'd just drop it. It's just how I operate now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you eat and when you eat it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say I have total control. Um... I decide exactly what I eat and when I eat it. Like I mentioned before, it's a strict routine I keep, so... I don't ever really feel out of control with food, if that's what you're asking. I just stick to my standard and that's it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to eat more if your job as a carpenter didn't require you to be so active and mobile?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I just know I still feel like I have weight to lose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than a healthy weight for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't think so. I mean... my husband and the apprentices at work keep saying I look too thin lately. But I just tell them I'm fine. I honestly think people are just confusing discipline with sickness. I work hard to keep myself in check, so no, I don't see it as being lower than a healthy weight at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see yourself as needing to lose weight if you were unable to see your body?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know. That's a hard scenario to imagine. \n\nI guess... well, I feel the weight on me, you know? Like, I can feel it when I'm moving around the job site. So maybe I would still feel like I needed to lose it, even if I couldn't actually look in a mirror. But honestly, I've never really thought about it. I mostly just go by what I see right now, and what I see tells me I'm not done yet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor is the one who sent me here. Um, my weight has dropped pretty low recently, and everyone is just making a big fuss over it. But honestly, I don't really see the issue. I feel like being this light helps me keep up with the work in my shop. It just keeps me from getting slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I do. I mean, my husband and the apprentices at the shop keep saying I look too thin, but I just tell them I'm fine. Honestly, I think people are just confusing discipline with sickness.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. If I eat anything heavy, or... let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying right away. I just worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, never. I really don't eat much at one time at all. Honestly, it's the exact opposite. I keep my portions very small so I don't feel weighed down while I'm working. The thought of eating a large amount of food like that just... no, I don't do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Since I eat so little to begin with, there really isn't any reason to do something like that. So, no, I've never made myself throw up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape has anything to do with your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean... I've never really looked at it that way. I've been a carpenter for a long time, and I'd say my worth comes from the hard work I do and being good at my trade. So, no, I haven't noticed my weight or my shape having anything to do with my actual self-worth as a person. It's really just a strict standard I keep for myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be able to stop dieting or restricting your food intake if you reached a weight that your doctor or family considered healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't think I would. It's not really about what someone else considers healthy, whether it's a doctor or my family. This is just the routine I need to maintain for myself. I don't see myself just stopping or changing how I eat because someone else picked a weight for me. Like I said, it's my own standard, so I don't think I'd just drop it. It's just how I operate now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you eat and when you eat it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say I have total control. Um... I decide exactly what I eat and when I eat it. Like I mentioned before, it's a strict routine I keep, so... I don't ever really feel out of control with food, if that's what you're asking. I just stick to my standard and that's it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to eat more if your job as a carpenter didn't require you to be so active and mobile?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, my job keeps me moving, sure, but... the way I eat isn't really tied to how many hours I spend on a ladder or hauling wood. Even if I were sitting at a desk all day, I'd still stick to the same routine. It's just the standard I've set for myself, so being less active wouldn't make me want to eat more. It really wouldn't change anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to lose weight even if you were at a weight that most people would consider healthy\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. It's... well, it's just that I can't really let go of it. If I eat anything heavy, or even let myself have the foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away. I just immediately worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep. So, no matter what weight I'm at or what other people think is healthy, that fear of gaining is always there. I'd still want to keep the weight off just to keep that control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to restrict your food intake if you weren't a carpenter and didn't have the physical demands of that job?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm sure I still would. Like I said before, my job doesn't really dictate how I eat. It's more about the routine I've set for myself. On workshop days, I usually just have tea and maybe a few careful bites of something. Stopping for a real meal just feels unnecessary, and honestly, it throws off the control I've got over the day. So, even if I weren't doing carpentry, I'd still be doing the exact same thing with my food.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I think they're really the same thing for me. Like I mentioned a few minutes ago, if I eat something heavy, or even think about having those rich foods we used to make for the holidays, I just... I start worrying right away that I'll gain weight. And if I gain weight, I feel like I'll lose that grip I've worked so hard to keep. So avoiding the weight gain is basically how I hold onto that control.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about not wanting to feel fat or more about maintaining a sense of control over your daily life?\n\nPatient: It's... well, it's really both, but they tie together. Like I said before, if I eat anything heavy, or if I even think about having those rich holiday foods we used to make, I just start worrying right away that I'm going to gain weight. And if I gain weight, it feels like I'm losing that grip I've worked so hard to keep. So the fear of gaining weight and the need for control are basically the same thing to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in your life, besides your weight, that you feel you need to have strict control over?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't think so. I mean, my work has to be precise, obviously, since I'm a carpenter. You have to measure things right and be careful. But that's just doing the job properly. \n\nOutside of that... no, I haven't really noticed anything else. It's really just the food and my weight that I feel like I have to keep that tight grip on. Everything else in my life is just, you know, normal. I don't feel that same need to control it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see yourself as overweight even if other people told you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, people don't really tell me I'm underweight, so I haven't really thought about it like that. I just look in the mirror and see what I see. I guess... I'm not sure if what other people say would change how I see myself. I just know I still feel like I have weight to lose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than a healthy weight for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't think so. I mean... my husband and the apprentices at work keep saying I look too thin lately. But I just tell them I'm fine. I honestly think people are just confusing discipline with sickness. I work hard to keep myself in check, so no, I don't see it as being lower than a healthy weight at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see yourself as needing to lose weight if you were unable to see your body?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know. That's a hard scenario to imagine. \n\nI guess... well, I feel the weight on me, you know? Like, I can feel it when I'm moving around the job site. So maybe I would still feel like I needed to lose it, even if I couldn't actually look in a mirror. But honestly, I've never really thought about it. I mostly just go by what I see right now, and what I see tells me I'm not done yet.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating primarily driven by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say it is. If I eat anything heavy, or... well, if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away. I just immediately think that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep. So, yeah, that fear is definitely what drives it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don't really know if I'd say it affects how I feel about myself as a person. I mean... I'm a good carpenter, I know my trade, and I take pride in my work. My weight doesn't change that.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor is the one who sent me here. Um, my weight has dropped pretty low recently, and everyone is just making a big fuss over it. But honestly, I don't really see the issue. I feel like being this light helps me keep up with the work in my shop. It just keeps me from getting slowed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your current weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I do. I mean, my husband and the apprentices at the shop keep saying I look too thin, but I just tell them I'm fine. Honestly, I think people are just confusing discipline with sickness.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. If I eat anything heavy, or... let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying right away. I just worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: No, never. I really don't eat much at one time at all. Honestly, it's the exact opposite. I keep my portions very small so I don't feel weighed down while I'm working. The thought of eating a large amount of food like that just... no, I don't do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't. Since I eat so little to begin with, there really isn't any reason to do something like that. So, no, I've never made myself throw up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape has anything to do with your self-worth?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean... I've never really looked at it that way. I've been a carpenter for a long time, and I'd say my worth comes from the hard work I do and being good at my trade. So, no, I haven't noticed my weight or my shape having anything to do with my actual self-worth as a person. It's really just a strict standard I keep for myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be able to stop dieting or restricting your food intake if you reached a weight that your doctor or family considered healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't think I would. It's not really about what someone else considers healthy, whether it's a doctor or my family. This is just the routine I need to maintain for myself. I don't see myself just stopping or changing how I eat because someone else picked a weight for me. Like I said, it's my own standard, so I don't think I'd just drop it. It's just how I operate now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you eat and when you eat it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say I have total control. Um... I decide exactly what I eat and when I eat it. Like I mentioned before, it's a strict routine I keep, so... I don't ever really feel out of control with food, if that's what you're asking. I just stick to my standard and that's it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to eat more if your job as a carpenter didn't require you to be so active and mobile?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, my job keeps me moving, sure, but... the way I eat isn't really tied to how many hours I spend on a ladder or hauling wood. Even if I were sitting at a desk all day, I'd still stick to the same routine. It's just the standard I've set for myself, so being less active wouldn't make me want to eat more. It really wouldn't change anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to lose weight even if you were at a weight that most people would consider healthy\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. It's... well, it's just that I can't really let go of it. If I eat anything heavy, or even let myself have the foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away. I just immediately worry that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep. So, no matter what weight I'm at or what other people think is healthy, that fear of gaining is always there. I'd still want to keep the weight off just to keep that control.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to restrict your food intake if you weren't a carpenter and didn't have the physical demands of that job?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'm sure I still would. Like I said before, my job doesn't really dictate how I eat. It's more about the routine I've set for myself. On workshop days, I usually just have tea and maybe a few careful bites of something. Stopping for a real meal just feels unnecessary, and honestly, it throws off the control I've got over the day. So, even if I weren't doing carpentry, I'd still be doing the exact same thing with my food.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I think they're really the same thing for me. Like I mentioned a few minutes ago, if I eat something heavy, or even think about having those rich foods we used to make for the holidays, I just... I start worrying right away that I'll gain weight. And if I gain weight, I feel like I'll lose that grip I've worked so hard to keep. So avoiding the weight gain is basically how I hold onto that control.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating more about not wanting to feel fat or more about maintaining a sense of control over your daily life?\n\nPatient: It's... well, it's really both, but they tie together. Like I said before, if I eat anything heavy, or if I even think about having those rich holiday foods we used to make, I just start worrying right away that I'm going to gain weight. And if I gain weight, it feels like I'm losing that grip I've worked so hard to keep. So the fear of gaining weight and the need for control are basically the same thing to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in your life, besides your weight, that you feel you need to have strict control over?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't think so. I mean, my work has to be precise, obviously, since I'm a carpenter. You have to measure things right and be careful. But that's just doing the job properly. \n\nOutside of that... no, I haven't really noticed anything else. It's really just the food and my weight that I feel like I have to keep that tight grip on. Everything else in my life is just, you know, normal. I don't feel that same need to control it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see yourself as overweight even if other people told you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I don't know. I mean, people don't really tell me I'm underweight, so I haven't really thought about it like that. I just look in the mirror and see what I see. I guess... I'm not sure if what other people say would change how I see myself. I just know I still feel like I have weight to lose.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than a healthy weight for someone of your height and build?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't think so. I mean... my husband and the apprentices at work keep saying I look too thin lately. But I just tell them I'm fine. I honestly think people are just confusing discipline with sickness. I work hard to keep myself in check, so no, I don't see it as being lower than a healthy weight at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still see yourself as needing to lose weight if you were unable to see your body?\n\nPatient: Um, I don't really know. That's a hard scenario to imagine. \n\nI guess... well, I feel the weight on me, you know? Like, I can feel it when I'm moving around the job site. So maybe I would still feel like I needed to lose it, even if I couldn't actually look in a mirror. But honestly, I've never really thought about it. I mostly just go by what I see right now, and what I see tells me I'm not done yet.\n\nDoctor: Is your restrictive eating primarily driven by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say it is. If I eat anything heavy, or... well, if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away. I just immediately think that I'll gain weight and lose the grip I've worked so hard to keep. So, yeah, that fear is definitely what drives it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body shape or weight affects how you feel about yourself as a person?\n\nPatient: I don't really know if I'd say it affects how I feel about myself as a person. I mean... I'm a good carpenter, I know my trade, and I take pride in my work. My weight doesn't change that. But I guess... I do feel better about myself when I'm sticking to my rules. When I stay disciplined, I feel like I'm in control, and that makes me feel good. But I wouldn't say my whole worth as a person is just about my shape.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
